\displaystyle=w(R)\frac{r^{2-p}}{p-2}.

That is,

\displaystyle-q(r)\leq\frac{w(R)}{p-2}r^{2-p}\iff q(r)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{w(R)}{p-2}r^{2-p}

\displaystyle=-\frac{R^{p-1}q^{\prime}(R)}{p-2}r^{2-p}

\displaystyle=cr^{2-p},c:=-\frac{R^{p-1}q^{\prime}(R)}{p-2}.

∎

S2.3
Proof of Lemma 2.3

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
r=||\boldsymbol{y}||
. Then the marginal density is given by,

m(\boldsymbol{y})=\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\leq\frac{r}{2}}\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}+\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}}\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}.

Note that if
||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\leq\frac{r}{2}
. Then,

||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta}||\geq|||\boldsymbol{y}||-||\boldsymbol{\theta}|||\geq\frac{r}{2},\text{ by the reverse triangle inequality}.

Therefore,
\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{r}{2})^{2}\}=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}\}
. This implies,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\leq\frac{r}{2}}\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}\}\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\leq\frac{r}{2}}\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}\}\cdot 1,\text{ since}\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\leq\frac{r}{2}}\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}\leq 1

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}\}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}}\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}}(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}}\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}).

Thus,

m(\boldsymbol{y})\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2}).

Now it remains to bound
\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2})
.
From Appendix A of
[
13
]
we know that

G_{p,q}^{q,0}(x|\begin{matrix}a_{1},\dots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\dots,b_{q}\end{matrix})\sim\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{2}}}{\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}}x^{{\theta}}\exp\{-\sigma x^{\frac{1}{\sigma}}\}\text{ as }x\rightarrow\infty,

(S2.1)

where
\sigma=q-p
and
{\theta}=\sigma^{-1}\{\frac{1-\sigma}{2}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{q}b_{i}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}\}
. Applying this result to a given Meijer-G function in the prior density gives,

G_{0,d}^{d,0}(\frac{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}}{B}|\begin{matrix}-\\
0,\frac{k_{1}-n_{d}}{2},\dots,\frac{k_{d-1}-n_{d}}{2})\end{matrix})\sim(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{\mu}\exp\{-dt^{\frac{1}{d}}\},\text{ as }t\rightarrow\infty

t=\frac{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}}{B},\mu=\frac{1}{d}(\frac{1-d}{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d-1}\frac{k_{j}-n_{d}}{2}),\text{ and }B=2^{d}\sigma_{1}^{2}\dots\sigma_{d}^{2}||\boldsymbol{x}||^{2}.

That is,

\frac{G_{0,d}^{d,0}(t|\begin{matrix}-\\
0,\frac{k_{1}-n_{d}}{2},\dots,\frac{k_{d-1}-n_{d}}{2}\end{matrix})}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}t^{\mu}\exp\{-dt^{\frac{1}{d}}\}}\rightarrow 1,\text{ as }t\rightarrow\infty.

By the definition of limit,

|\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}-1|<\epsilon.

Let
\epsilon=1
. Then,

-1<\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}-1<1.

This implies,

0<\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}<2.

Hence, eventually
f(t)\leq 2g(t)
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle G_{0,d}^{d,0}(t|\begin{matrix}-\\
0,\frac{k_{1}-n_{d}}{2},\dots,\frac{k_{d-1}-n_{d}}{2}\end{matrix})

\displaystyle\leq 2Ct^{\mu}\exp\{-dt^{\frac{1}{d}}\},,\text{ where }C=(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=Kt^{\mu}\exp\{-d{t}^{\frac{1}{d}}\},K=2C

\displaystyle=K(\frac{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}}{B})^{\mu}\exp\{-d(\frac{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}}{B})^{\frac{1}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=KB^{-\mu}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu}\exp\{-dB^{-\frac{1}{d}}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=K_{\mu}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu}\exp\{-c||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{\frac{2}{d}}\},c:=dB^{-\frac{1}{d}},K_{\mu}:=KB^{-\mu}.

Substituting this upper bound into the prior density gives,

\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\frac{1}{2^{n_{1}+\dots n_{d-1}}}\sum\limits_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\dots\sum\limits_{k_{d-1}=1}^{n_{d-1}}\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{\ell}}{2})}}{(\pi B)^{\frac{n_{d}}{2}}}k_{\mu(k_{1},...,k_{d-1})}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu(k_{1},..k_{d-1})}\exp\{-c||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{\frac{2}{d}}\}.

Let
\mu_{\text{max}}=\max\limits_{1\leq k_{1}\leq n_{1},\dots,1\leq k_{d-1}\leq n_{d-1}}\mu(k_{1},\dots,k_{d-1})=\mu(n_{1},\dots,n_{d-1})
. Since
t\rightarrow\infty
, we know
\boldsymbol{\theta}\rightarrow\infty
, so
||\boldsymbol{\theta}||
is large. Therefore,
||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu_{\text{max}}}
is increasing in
\mu
. Since
\mu(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\leq\mu_{\text{max}}
for every possible
k_{1},\dots,k_{d},||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu(k_{1},...,k_{d-1})}\leq||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu_{\text{max}}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu_{\text{max}}}\exp\{-c||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{2^{n_{1}+\dots n_{d-1}}}\sum\limits_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\dots\sum\limits_{k_{d-1}}^{n_{d-1}}\begin{pmatrix}n_{1}\\
k_{1}\end{pmatrix}\dots\begin{pmatrix}n_{d-1}\\
k_{d-1}\end{pmatrix}\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{\ell}}{2})}}{(2^{d}\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}\dots\sigma_{d}^{2}||\boldsymbol{x}||^{2})^{\frac{n_{d}}{2}}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2\mu_{\text{max}}}\exp\{-c||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{\frac{2}{d}}\},\text{ where}

C_{1}:=\frac{1}{2^{n_{1}+\dots n_{d-1}}}\sum\limits_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\dots\sum\limits_{k_{d-1}}^{n_{d-1}}\begin{pmatrix}n_{1}\\
k_{1}\end{pmatrix}\dots\begin{pmatrix}n_{d-1}\\
k_{d-1}\end{pmatrix}\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{k_{\ell}}{2})}}{(\pi B)^{\frac{n_{d}}{2}}}

Now, let
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\rho\boldsymbol{\omega}
, where
\rho=||\boldsymbol{\theta}||\in(0,\infty),\boldsymbol{\omega}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{||\boldsymbol{\theta}||}\in\mathcal{S}^{p-1}
,the unit sphere with
d\boldsymbol{\theta}=\rho^{p-1}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\rho
where
d\boldsymbol{\omega}
is the surface measure on
\mathcal{S}^{p-1}
. Then the integral becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>u)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{S}^{p-1}}\pi(\rho\boldsymbol{\omega})\rho^{p-1}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\rho.

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\rho^{p-1}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}^{p-1}}\pi(\rho\boldsymbol{\omega})d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\rho

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\rho^{p-1}\tilde{\pi}(\rho)\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}^{p-1}}d\boldsymbol{\omega}d\rho,\text{ as }\tilde{\pi}(\rho)=\pi(\rho\boldsymbol{\omega})\text{ is radial}

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{S}^{p-1}|\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\rho^{p-1}\tilde{\pi}(\rho)d\rho

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{S}^{p-1}|C_{1}\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\rho^{p-1}\rho^{2\mu_{\text{max}}}\exp\{-c\rho^{\frac{2}{d}}\}d\rho

\displaystyle=K_{2}\int\limits_{u}^{\infty}\rho^{p-1+2\mu_{\text{max}}}\exp\{-c\rho^{\frac{2}{d}}\}d\rho,\text{ where }K_{2}=|\mathcal{S}^{p-1}|C_{1}.

Let
\nu=\rho^{\frac{2}{d}}\implies\rho=\nu^{\frac{d}{2}}\implies\frac{d\rho}{d\nu}=\frac{d}{2}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\implies d\rho=\frac{d}{2}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}d\nu
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>u)

\displaystyle\leq K_{2}\int\limits_{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}^{\infty}(\nu^{\frac{d}{2}})^{p-1+2\mu_{\text{max}}}\exp\{-c(\nu^{\frac{d}{2}})^{\frac{2}{d}}\}\frac{d}{2}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}d\nu

\displaystyle=K_{3}\int\limits_{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}^{\infty}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}(p-1+2\mu_{\text{max}})}\exp\{-c\nu\}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}d\nu,K_{3}=\frac{K_{2}d}{2}

\displaystyle=K_{3}\int\limits_{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}^{\infty}\nu^{\frac{d}{2}(p+2\mu_{\text{max}})-1}\exp\{-c\nu\}d\nu

\displaystyle=K_{3}\int\limits_{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}^{\infty}\nu^{A-1}\exp\{-c\nu\}d\nu,A:=\frac{d}{2}(p+2\mu_{\max})

\displaystyle\leq K_{3}C_{A}(u^{\frac{2}{d}})^{A-1}\exp\{-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\},\text{ for }C_{A}>0\text{ and large }u^{\frac{2}{d}},

since under such conditions,

\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-1}e^{-c\nu}d\nu\leq C_{A}x^{A-1}e^{-cx}.

Particularly, let
I(x):=\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-1}e^{-c\nu}d\nu,A>1,c>0
. Let

u=\nu^{A-1}\text{ and }\frac{dv}{d\nu}=e^{-c\nu}.

Then,

du=(A-1)\nu^{A-2}d\nu\text{ and }v=\int e^{-c\nu}d\nu=-\frac{1}{c}e^{-c\nu}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle I(x)

\displaystyle=[-\frac{1}{c}\nu^{A-1}e^{-c\nu}]_{x}^{\infty}+\frac{A-1}{c}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-2}e^{-c\nu}d\nu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}x^{A-1}e^{-cx}+\frac{A-1}{c}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-2}e^{-c\nu}d\nu.

For
\nu>x,\nu^{A-2}=\nu^{-1}\nu^{A-1}\leq x^{-1}\nu^{A-1}
. This implies

\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-2}e^{-c\nu}d\nu\leq\frac{1}{x}\int\limits_{x}^{\infty}\nu^{A-1}e^{-c\nu}d\nu=\frac{1}{x}I(x).

Therefore,

I(x)\leq\frac{1}{c}x^{A-1}e^{-cx}+\frac{A-1}{c}\cdot\frac{1}{x}I(x).

Hence,

(1-\frac{A-1}{cx})I(x)\leq\frac{1}{c}x^{A-1}e^{-cx}.

So for large
x
,
I(x)\leq C_{A}x^{A-1}e^{-cx}
for some
C_{A}>0
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>u)\leq K_{4}u^{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)}\exp\{-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\},K_{4}:=K_{3}C_{A}.

Now,

\displaystyle u^{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)}\exp\{-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=\exp\{\log(u^{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)})\}\exp\{-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=\exp\{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\}.

Note that

\frac{\log(u)}{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }u\rightarrow\infty.

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,c)
and let

\delta:=\frac{\epsilon}{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)}.

By the definition of limit

\displaystyle\frac{\log(u)}{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}\leq\delta

\displaystyle\iff\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\frac{\log(u)}{u^{\frac{2}{d}}}\leq\epsilon

\displaystyle\iff\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)\leq\epsilon u^{\frac{2}{d}}

\displaystyle\iff-\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)\geq-\epsilon u^{\frac{2}{d}}

\displaystyle\iff cu^{\frac{2}{d}}-\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)\geq cu^{\frac{2}{d}}-\epsilon u^{\frac{2}{d}}

\displaystyle\iff cu^{\frac{2}{d}}-\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)\geq(c-\epsilon)u^{\frac{2}{d}}.

So,
\forall u\geq u_{0}
for some
u_{0}
,

cu^{\frac{2}{d}}-\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)\geq(c-\epsilon)u^{\frac{2}{d}}.

Therefore,

\frac{2}{d}(A-1)\log(u)-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\leq-(c-\epsilon)u^{\frac{2}{d}}.

Thus,

u^{\frac{2}{d}(A-1)}\exp\{-cu^{\frac{2}{d}}\}\leq\exp\{-(c-\epsilon)u^{\frac{2}{d}}\},u\geq u_{0}.

This implies,

\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>u)\leq K_{4}\exp\{-c^{\prime}u^{\frac{2}{d}}\},\text{ where }c^{\prime}=c-\epsilon.

This gives,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(||\boldsymbol{\theta}||>\frac{r}{2})

\displaystyle\leq K_{4}\exp\{-c^{\prime}(\frac{r}{2})^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=K_{4}\exp\{-c^{\prime\prime}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\},c^{\prime\prime}=c^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2}{d}}.

Then, for large
r
,

m(\boldsymbol{y})\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}K_{4}\exp\{-c^{\prime\prime}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}.

For
r,d\geq 1,e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{8}}\leq e^{-\frac{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}{8}}
. This implies,

m(\boldsymbol{y})\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}{8}\}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}K_{4}\exp\{-c^{\prime\prime}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

Let
\kappa:=\min\{\frac{1}{8},c^{\prime\prime}\}
. Then,

\displaystyle m(\boldsymbol{y})

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\kappa r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}K_{4}\exp\{-\kappa r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=[(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}K_{4}]\exp\{-\kappa r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}

\displaystyle=K_{5}\exp\{-\kappa r^{\frac{2}{d}}\},K_{5}:=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}+(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}K_{4}

∎

S2.4
Proof of Theorem 2.4

Proof.

Let
q:=\sqrt{m(\boldsymbol{y})}
. Assume
q
is superharmonic.

Since
q
is radial, this implies
\exists R>0
such that
\forall r\geq R

Q^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{p-1}{r}Q^{\prime}(r)\leq 0,Q(r)=q(\boldsymbol{y}),\text{ where }r=||\boldsymbol{y}||,\text{ in view of Lemma 2.2.}

Note
Q(r)\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, we can apply Lemma 2.2. That is,
\exists c>0
such that
\forall r\geq R,Q(r)\geq cr^{2-p}
. By lemma 2.3,
\exists C>0,\kappa>0,R_{0}>0
such that
\forall r\geq R_{0},Q(r)\leq\sqrt{C}\exp\{-\frac{\kappa}{2}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}
. Thus,

\forall r\geq\max\{R,R_{0}\},cr^{2-p}\leq\sqrt{C}\exp\{-\frac{\kappa}{2}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\}\text{ iff }\log(c)+(2-p)\log(r)\leq\log(\sqrt{C})-\frac{\kappa}{2}r^{\frac{2}{d}}.

That is,

\frac{\kappa}{2}r^{\frac{2}{d}}\leq\log(\frac{\sqrt{C}}{c})+(p-2)\log(r).

This gives,

\frac{\kappa}{2}\leq\frac{\log(\frac{\sqrt{C}}{c})}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}+(p-2)\frac{\log(r)}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}.

As
r\rightarrow\infty
,
\frac{\log(\frac{\sqrt{C}}{c})}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}\rightarrow 0
and
\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log(r)}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}=\lim\limits_{r\rightarrow\infty}\frac{d}{2r^{\frac{2}{d}}}\rightarrow 0
, by L’Hôpital’s rule. Therefore, for sufficiently large r,

\frac{\log(\frac{\sqrt{C}}{c})}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}+(p-2)\frac{\log(r)}{r^{\frac{2}{d}}}<\frac{\kappa}{2}.

This is a contradiction. Therefore
q
is not superharmonic.
∎

S2.5
Proof of Theorem 2.5

Proof.

Under quadratic loss, the Bayes estimator is the posterior mean. So,

\delta_{\text{BNN,fixed}}(\boldsymbol{y})=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}].

Thus it suffices to compute the posterior mean. Fix
V=v
. Then the model becomes

\boldsymbol{Y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim N_{p}(\boldsymbol{\theta},I_{p})\text{ and }\boldsymbol{\theta}|V=v\sim N_{p}(\boldsymbol{0}_{p},vI_{p}).

Hence,

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{y},v)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\theta}|v)}{p(\boldsymbol{y}|v)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta},v)p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|v)}{p(\boldsymbol{y}|v)}

\displaystyle\propto p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta})p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|v)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}\}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2v}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}\}

\displaystyle=\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}-\frac{1}{2v}||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}\}

\displaystyle=\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}[||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}-2\boldsymbol{y}^{T}\boldsymbol{\theta}+(1+\frac{1}{v})||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}]\}.

Now complete the square. Let
a:=1+v^{-1}
. Then the expression becomes

a||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}-2\boldsymbol{y}^{T}\boldsymbol{\theta}+||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}.

We want to write this as
a||\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{m}||^{2}+
a constant, where
\boldsymbol{m}
and the constant need to be determined. Expanding gives

a||\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{m}||^{2}=a||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}-2a\boldsymbol{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\theta}+a||\boldsymbol{m}||^{2}.

Matching terms gives
a\boldsymbol{m}=\boldsymbol{y}
which implies
\boldsymbol{m}=a^{-1}\boldsymbol{y}=(v/1+v)\boldsymbol{y}
. The constant term does not depend on
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and can be absorbed into the constant of proportionality. Then,

p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{y},v)\propto\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\frac{1}{v})||\boldsymbol{\theta}-\frac{v}{1+v}\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}\}.

This implies

\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y},V=v\sim N_{p}(\frac{v}{1+v}\boldsymbol{y},\frac{v}{1+v}I_{p}).

Then,

\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y},V=v]=\frac{v}{1+v}\boldsymbol{y}.

Then by the law of total expectation,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y},V=v]|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{1+V}\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{1+V}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}]\boldsymbol{y}.

Observe that

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{y}|v)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}p(\boldsymbol{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta},v)p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|v)d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{\theta};\boldsymbol{0}_{p},I_{p})\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{\theta};\boldsymbol{0},vI_{p})d\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\phi_{p}(\boldsymbol{y};\boldsymbol{0}_{p},(1+v)I_{p}),\text{ by convolution of Gaussians}.

That is,
\boldsymbol{Y}|V=v\sim N_{p}(\boldsymbol{0}_{p},(1+v)I_{p})
. Then,

p(\boldsymbol{y}|v)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{p}{2}}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}}{2(1+v)}\}.

By Bayes’ theorem,

p(v|\boldsymbol{y})\propto(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}}{2(1+v)}\}\pi_{V}(dv),

which depends on
\boldsymbol{y}
only through
||\boldsymbol{y}||^{{2}}
. Hence,

\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{1+V}|\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{y}]=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{1+V}|||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}=||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}].

Therefore,

\delta_{\text{BNN, fixed}}(\boldsymbol{y})=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{1+V}|||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}=||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}]\boldsymbol{y}.

∎

S2.6
Proof of Theorem 2.6

Proof.

We know that

\delta(\boldsymbol{Y})=\delta_{\text{BNN,fixed}}(\boldsymbol{Y})=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{V+1}|||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}]\boldsymbol{Y}.

Let
U:=||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}Y_{i}^{2}
. Then,

a(u):=\mathbb{E}[\frac{V}{V+1}|U=u]\text{ and }\psi(u):=1-a(u)=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{V+1}|U=u].

Therefore,

\delta(\boldsymbol{y})=(1-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}))\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{y}},\text{ with }0<\psi(u)<1.

Let
\pi(v)
denote the unconditional density of
V
. Let

g(\boldsymbol{y})=\delta(\boldsymbol{y})-\boldsymbol{y}=-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})\boldsymbol{y}

Then,

\displaystyle||g(\boldsymbol{y})||

\displaystyle=||-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})\boldsymbol{y}||

\displaystyle=|-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})|||\boldsymbol{y}||

\displaystyle=\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})||\boldsymbol{y}||

\displaystyle\leq||\boldsymbol{y}||,\text{ since }0<\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})<1.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[||g(\boldsymbol{Y})||^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}Y_{i}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[Y_{i}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}1+\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=p+||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}<\infty.

Let
\Sigma_{V}:=(1+V)I_{p}
. Since
\boldsymbol{Y}|V=v\sim N_{p}(\boldsymbol{0}_{p},\Sigma_{v})
we know that
\boldsymbol{Y}|V=v\overset{d}{=}\sqrt{1+v}\boldsymbol{Z}
,
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim N_{p}(\boldsymbol{0}_{p},I_{p}).
Then,
U=||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}=||\sqrt{1+v}\boldsymbol{Z}||^{2}=|\sqrt{1+v}|^{2}||\boldsymbol{Z}||^{2}=(1+v)W,
where
W:=||\boldsymbol{Z}||^{2}
. That is,
U|V=v\overset{d}{=}(1+v)W
. Since
u=(1+v)w
we know

w=\frac{u}{1+v}\implies\frac{dw}{du}=\frac{1}{1+v}.

Then,

\displaystyle f_{U|V=v}(u|v)

\displaystyle=f_{W}(\frac{u}{1+v})|\frac{1}{1+v}|,\text{ where }W\overset{d}{=}\chi_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+v}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{p}{2})}(\frac{u}{1+v})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{p}{2})}u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}

\displaystyle\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{V|U=u}(v|u)

\displaystyle\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\pi(v)

\displaystyle\propto(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\pi(v).

Let

\displaystyle D(u):=\mathbb{E}[(1+V)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+V)}\}]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\pi(v)(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}dv

and

\displaystyle N(u):=\mathbb{E}[(1+V)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+V)}\}]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\pi(v)(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}dv.

Then

f_{V|U=u}(v|u)=\frac{1}{D(u)}\pi(v)(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}.

Recall

\displaystyle\psi(u)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{V+1}|U=u]

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+v}\pi(v|u)dv

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D(u)}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+v}\pi(v)(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}dv

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D(u)}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\pi(v)(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}dv

\displaystyle=\frac{N(u)}{D(u)}.

Let

f(u,v):=(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}.

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\{(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\}

\displaystyle=(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\{\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\}

\displaystyle=(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}(-\frac{1}{2(1+v)})\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}.

For
h\neq 0
,

\frac{D(u+h)-D(u)}{h}=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f(u+h,v)-f(u,v)}{h}\mu(dv).

For fixed
v
, apply the single variable mean value theorem to
t\rightarrow f(t,v)
on the interval between
u
and
u+h
,
\exists\xi=\xi(h,v)\in(0,1)
such that

\frac{f(u+h,v)-f(u,v)}{h}=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u+\xi h,v).

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\frac{f(u+h,v)-f(u,v)}{h}|

\displaystyle=|\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u+\xi h,v)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u+\xi h}{2(1+v)}\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}.

Note that

g(v):=\frac{1}{2}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}

is
\mu-
integrable. Particularly
0\leq(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\leq 1,\forall v\geq 0
. Therefore,

\int g(v)\mu(dv)\leq\frac{1}{2}\int 1\mu(dv)\leq\frac{1}{2}<\infty.\text{ Since }\lim\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(u+h,v)-f(u,v)}{h}\rightarrow\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u,v),

by the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{D(u+h)-D(u)}{h}

\displaystyle=\int\lim\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(u+h,v)-f(u,v)}{h}\mu(dv)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial u}f(u,v)\mu(dv)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\mu(dv).

That is,

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(1+V)^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+V)}\}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}N(u).

Similarly,

M(u):=\mathbb{E}[(1+V)^{-\frac{p}{2}-2}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+V)}\}],\text{ with }N^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{1}{2}M(u).

Then,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{du}\{N(u)D(u)^{-1}\}

\displaystyle=D(u)^{-1}N^{\prime}(u)+N(u)[-D(u)^{-2}D^{\prime}(u)]

\displaystyle=\frac{N^{\prime}(u)}{D(u)}-\frac{N(u)D^{\prime}(u)}{D(u)^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{M(u)}{D(u)}+\frac{1}{2}(\frac{N(u)}{D(u)})^{2}.

Observe that
\mathbb{P}(U\in du,V\in dv)=f_{U|V=v}(u|v)du\mu(dv)
. Then,

\mu(dv|u)=\frac{f_{U|V=v}(u|v)\mu(dv)}{\int f_{U|V=v}(u|t)\mu(dt)}.

Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\frac{1}{1+V})^{2}|U=u]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{(1+v)^{2}}\mu(dv|u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int(1+v)^{-2}f_{U|V=v}(u|v)\mu(dv)}{\int f_{U|V=v}(u|t)\mu(dt)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{p}{2})}\int(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}-2}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}\mu(dv)}{\frac{u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}}{2^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma(\frac{p}{2})}\int(1+t)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+t)}\}\mu(dt)}

\displaystyle=\frac{M(u)}{D(u)}.

Similarly

\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u]=\frac{N(u)}{D(u)}=\psi(u).

Therefore

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{E}[(\frac{1}{1+V})^{2}|U=u]-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u]^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}Var(\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u)\leq 0.

This means

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq Var(\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\frac{1}{1+V})^{2}|U=u]-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[(\frac{1}{1+V})^{2}|U=u]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u],\text{ since }\frac{1}{1+V}\in(0,1]

\displaystyle=\psi(u).

This implies

\displaystyle-Var(\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u)\geq-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u]

\displaystyle\iff-\frac{1}{2}Var(\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u)\geq-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{1+V}|U=u]

\displaystyle\iff\psi^{\prime}(u)\geq-\frac{1}{2}\psi(u).

Therefore,

0\geq\psi^{\prime}(u)\geq-\frac{1}{2}\psi(u),\text{ where }\psi(u)\leq 1.

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\{-\psi(u)y_{i}\}

\displaystyle=y_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\{-\psi(u)\}+(-\psi(u))\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}\{y_{i}\}

\displaystyle=-y_{i}\frac{\partial\psi(u)}{\partial y_{i}}-\psi(u)

\displaystyle=-y_{i}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial u}\frac{\partial u}{\partial y_{i}}-\psi(u)

\displaystyle=-y_{i}\psi^{\prime}(u)(2y_{i})-\psi(u)

\displaystyle=-2y_{i}^{2}\psi^{\prime}(u)-\psi(u).

Therefore,

\displaystyle div(g(\boldsymbol{y}))=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\partial g_{i}}{\partial y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}[-\psi(u)-2y_{i}^{2}\psi^{\prime}(u)]

\displaystyle=-p\psi(u)-2\psi^{\prime}(u)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p}y_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=-p\psi(u)-2||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}\psi^{\prime}(u).

So,

|\nabla\cdot g(\boldsymbol{y})|\leq p|\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})|+2||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}|\psi^{\prime}(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})|\leq p+||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}.

Hence,

\mathbb{E}_{\theta}|\nabla\cdot g(\boldsymbol{Y})|\leq p+\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[||\boldsymbol{Y}||^{2}]=2p+||\boldsymbol{\theta}||^{2}<\infty.

Recall
\delta(\boldsymbol{y})=g(\boldsymbol{y})+\boldsymbol{y}
with
g(\boldsymbol{y})=-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})\boldsymbol{y}
. From above we know
0\geq\psi^{\prime}(u)\geq-\frac{1}{2}
, that is
|\psi^{\prime}(u)|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. From above we know that
g(\boldsymbol{y})
is differentiable and thus also weakly differentiable. Therefore we may use Stein’s unbiased risk estimator (
[
25
]
). Furthermore,

\displaystyle||g(\boldsymbol{y})||^{2}

\displaystyle=||-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}

\displaystyle=||-\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})||^{2}||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}

\displaystyle=||\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})||^{2}||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}

\displaystyle=\psi(||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2})^{2}||\boldsymbol{y}||^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle R(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\delta})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[p+||g(\boldsymbol{Y})||^{2}+2div(g(\boldsymbol{Y}))]

\displaystyle=p+\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[\psi(U)^{2}U-2p\psi(U)-4U\psi^{\prime}(U)].

Then the excess risk integrand is given by,

B(u):=\psi(u)^{2}u-2p\psi(u)-4u\psi^{\prime}(u).

Then
R(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\delta})=p+\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}[B(U)]
. From above
\psi^{\prime}(u)\leq 0
. Therefore
-4u\psi^{\prime}(u)\geq 0
. Thus,

B(u)\geq\psi(u)^{2}u-2p\psi(u)=\psi(u)(u\psi(u)-2p).

Therefore, whenever
u\psi(u)>2p
we have
B(u)>0
. If we can show that
u\psi(u)\rightarrow\infty
as
u\rightarrow\infty
then we can deduce non-minimaxity. Fix
\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d-1})
. Conditional on
\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k},V=C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}T_{\ell},T_{\ell}|\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k}\sim\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell},1),\alpha_{\ell}=k_{\ell}/2
.
Recall,

\displaystyle f_{U|V=v}(u|v)

\displaystyle\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+v)^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+v)}\}

and

\displaystyle f_{U|T=t,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k}}(u|t,\boldsymbol{k})

\displaystyle\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})}\}.

By Bayes’ theorem,

\pi(t|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k})=\frac{f_{U|t,\boldsymbol{k}}(u|t,\boldsymbol{k})\pi_{T|\boldsymbol{K}}(t|\boldsymbol{k})}{m_{U|\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k}}(u|\boldsymbol{k})},m_{U|\boldsymbol{K}}(u)=\int f_{U|t,\boldsymbol{k}}(u|t,\boldsymbol{k})\pi_{T|\boldsymbol{K}}(t|\boldsymbol{k})dt.

That is
\pi(t|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k})\propto f_{U|t,\boldsymbol{k}}(u|t,\boldsymbol{k})\pi_{T|\boldsymbol{K}}(t)
. Therefore,

\pi(t|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k})\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})}\}\pi_{T|\boldsymbol{K}}(t).

Now,

\displaystyle\pi_{T_{1},\dots,T_{d-1}|\boldsymbol{K}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{d-1}|\boldsymbol{k})

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}f_{T_{\ell}|{K}_{\ell}}(t_{\ell}|k_{\ell}),\text{ by independence},

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell})}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}\exp\{-t_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell})^{-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})\exp\{-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle\propto(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})\exp\{-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\pi(t|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k})

\displaystyle\propto u^{\frac{p}{2}-1}(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})}\}

\displaystyle\times(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})\exp\{-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle\propto(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{-\frac{p}{2}}(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})\exp\{-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}-\frac{u}{2(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})}\}.

Let
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{d-1})\in(0,\infty)^{d-1}.
Let
x=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
. Then
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}=x^{d-1}
. Now
s_{j}:=x^{-1}t_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,d-1
. Then,
t_{j}=xs_{j}
. This implies

\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\frac{t_{\ell}}{x}=\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}}{x^{d-1}}=\frac{x^{d-1}}{x^{d-1}}=1.

Therefore,
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}=1
. This means not all the
s_{j}
’s are independent. We will keep
s_{1},\dots,s_{d-2}
free and define
s_{d-1}:=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}
. Then
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})s_{d-1}=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}
which equals
1
. In summary we have
\boldsymbol{t}\rightarrow(x,s_{1},\dots,s_{d-2})
where
x=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
,
s_{i}=x^{-1}t_{i},i=1,\dots,d-2
. The inverse map is given by
(x,s_{1},\dots,s_{d-2})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{t}
where
t_{i}=xs_{i},i=1,\dots,d-2,t_{d-1}=xs_{d-1}=x(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}
. Let
r:=\log(x),u_{i}:=\log(s_{i}),

i=1,\dots,d-2
. Then

s_{d-1}=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}\implies\log(s_{d-1})=\log(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}=-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\log(s_{\ell})=-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}u_{\ell}.

Now
z_{j}:=\log(t_{j})=\log(xs_{j})=\log(x)+\log(s_{j})=r+u_{j},j=1,\dots,d-2
. Then
z_{d-1}=r-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}u_{\ell}
. Let

A=\frac{\partial(z_{1},\dots,z_{d-1})}{\partial(r,u_{1},\dots,u_{d-2})}.

Then for
i\leq d-2,

\frac{\partial z_{i}}{\partial r}=1,\frac{\partial z_{i}}{\partial u_{i}}=1,\text{ and }\frac{\partial z_{i}}{\partial u_{j}}=0\text{ for }i\neq j.

Then row
i
of A is given by
[1,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0]
where the second
1
is in the column corresponding to
u_{i}
. For row
d-1
of A,

\frac{\partial z_{d-1}}{\partial r}=1\text{ and }\frac{\partial z_{d-1}}{\partial u_{j}}=-1\text{ for }j=1,\dots,d-2.

So the last row of A is
[1,-1,\dots,-1]
. Then,

A=\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0&0&\dots&0\\
1&0&1&0&\dots&0\\
1&0&0&1&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
1&0&0&0&\dots&1\\
1&-1&-1&-1&\dots&-1\end{bmatrix}.

Now
R_{2},\dots,R_{d-1}\rightarrow R_{2},\dots,R_{d-1}-R_{1}
. Then
R_{2},\dots,R_{d-2}=[0,-1,1,0,\dots,0]
with a
-1
in the column corresponding to
u_{1}
and a 1 in column
u_{i},i=2,\dots,d-2
. Furthermore,
R_{d-1}=[0,-2,-1,\dots,-1]
. Then the transformed matrix is given by

A^{{}^{\prime}}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&-1&1&0&\dots&0\\
0&-1&0&1&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&-2&-1&-1&\dots&-1\end{bmatrix}.

Then
\det(A)=\det(A^{{}^{\prime}})
. We can use Laplace expansion by deleting row
1
and column
1
. This gives,

B=\begin{bmatrix}-1&1&0&0&\dots&0\\
-1&0&1&0&\dots&0\\
-1&0&0&1&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
-1&0&0&0&\dots&1\\
-2&-1&-1&-1&\dots&-1\end{bmatrix}.

Now
R_{d-2}\rightarrow R_{d-2}+R_{1}+R_{2}+\dots+R_{d-3}.
So in column 1 row
d-2
,
-2+(d-3)(-1)=-2-(d-3)=-2-d+3=-(d-1)
. Then column
2
to column
d-2
we have
-1+1=0
. Therefore the last row becomes
(-(d-1),0,0,\dots,0)
. So the transformed B matrix is lower triangular except the last row. Therefore the determinant is equal to
|\det(A)|=d-1
. We have
t_{j}=e^{z_{j}}
. This gives

\frac{dt_{j}}{dz_{j}}=e^{z_{j}}\text{ i.e }dt_{j}=e^{z_{j}}dz_{j}.

Then

\displaystyle dt_{1}\dots dt_{d-1}

\displaystyle=(e^{z_{1}}dz_{1})(e^{z_{2}}dz_{2})\dots(e^{z_{d-1}}dz_{d-1})

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d-1}e^{z_{j}})dz_{1}\dots dz_{d-1}

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d-1}t_{j})dz_{1}\dots dz_{d-1}.

So

dz_{1}\dots dz_{d-1}=|\det(A)|drdu_{1}\dots du_{d-2}=(d-1)drdu_{1}\dots du_{d-2}

We also know
dr=x^{-1}dx
and
du_{i}=s_{i}^{-1}ds_{i}
. Then

dt_{1}\dots dt_{d-1}=x^{d-1}(d-1)x^{-1}dx\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d-2}s_{j}^{-1}ds_{j}=(d-1)x^{d-2}(\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d-2}s_{j}^{-1})dxds_{1}\dots ds_{d-2}.

Now we can rewrite the kernel of
p(t|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k})
. Observe

(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})^{-\frac{p}{2}}=(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\text{ and }\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+C\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell})}\}=\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}.

Furthermore,
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}(xs_{\ell})^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}=x^{\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}(\alpha_{\ell}-1)}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}
. Let
\alpha_{\cdot}=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{\ell}
. Then
\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}(\alpha_{\ell}-1)=\alpha_{\cdot}-(d-1)
. So,
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}=x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-(d-1)}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}
. Finally

\exp\{-\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}t_{\ell}\}=\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})\}
, where
A(\boldsymbol{s}):=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}
. Now,

\displaystyle\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x,\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle\propto\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(t(x,\boldsymbol{s}))\cdot(d-1)x^{d-2}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1}

\displaystyle\propto(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-(d-1)}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})\}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}

\displaystyle\times(d-1)x^{d-2}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1}

\displaystyle\propto(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-d+1+d-2}

\displaystyle\times(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1}).

Let

\displaystyle Q(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle:=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})s_{d-1}^{\alpha_{d-1}-1}(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})[(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}]^{\alpha_{d-1}-1}(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-(\alpha_{d-1}-1)}(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-(\alpha_{d-1}-1)})(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-1}s_{\ell}^{-\alpha_{d-1}+1}s_{\ell}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{d-1}-1}.

Therefore,

\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x,\boldsymbol{s})\propto(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}Q(\boldsymbol{s}).

Then,

\displaystyle\pi(x|u,\boldsymbol{k})

\displaystyle\propto\int(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})\}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}\int\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})\}Q(\boldsymbol{s})ds

\displaystyle=(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}H(x),

where

H(x):=\int e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}.

Now we need to understand each term in
H(x)
. Firstly we will look at
A(\boldsymbol{s})
. Particularly,

A(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}+s_{d-1}=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}+(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}.

By the AM-GM inequality

\frac{1}{d-1}\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}\geq(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell})^{\frac{1}{d-1}}=1.

That is
A(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}\geq d-1
.Note that equality occurs when
s_{1}=\dots=s_{d-1}=\eta
. Then
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-1}s_{\ell}=\eta^{d-1}=1\implies\eta=1
. Therefore,
s_{1}=\dots=s_{d-1}=1
. Therefore
A(\boldsymbol{s})
has a unique global minimizer at
\boldsymbol{s}^{*}=(1,\dots,1)\in(0,\infty)^{d-2}
with
A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})=d-1.
Observe that for
i\in\{1,\dots,d-2\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\{(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}\}

\displaystyle=-(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-2}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle=-(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-2}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\{s_{i}\prod\limits_{\ell\neq i}^{d-2}s_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle=-(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-2}\prod\limits_{\ell\neq i}^{d-2}s_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-2}s_{i}^{-1}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}}{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}}.

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\{-s_{i}^{-1}s_{d-1}\}

\displaystyle=-s_{i}^{-1}\frac{\partial s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{i}}+s_{d-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}\{-s_{i}^{-1}\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{s_{i}}(-\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}})+s_{d-1}(s_{i}^{-2})

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}^{2}}+\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2s_{d-1}}{s_{i}^{2}}.

Furthermore,

\frac{\partial^{2}s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{i}\partial s_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}\{-\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}}\}=-\frac{1}{s_{i}}\frac{\partial s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{j}}=-\frac{1}{s_{i}}(-\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{j}})=\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}s_{j}}.

Then

\frac{\partial A}{\partial s_{i}}=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{i}}s_{\ell}+\frac{\partial s_{d-1}}{\partial s_{i}}=1-\frac{s_{d-1}}{s_{i}}.

Then at
\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
we have
\nabla A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})=0
. Then at
\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
we have
\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})=I_{d-2}+\boldsymbol{1}\boldsymbol{1}^{T}
. The eigenvalues of
\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})
are 1 with multiplicity
d-3
and
d-1
with multiplicity 1. Therefore
\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})
is positive definite. Let
w_{i}:=s_{i}^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{d-2})^{T}
. Then the matrix
\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}
has entries
w_{i}w_{j}=(s_{i}s_{j})^{-1}
. Furthermore
\text{diag}(w_{i}^{2})
has entries
s_{i}^{-2}
. Then

\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{d-1}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}+\text{diag}(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2})).

We know
\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}
is positive semi-definite and
diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2})
is positive definite. Then
\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})
is positive definite for every
\boldsymbol{s}\in(0,\infty)^{d-2}
. Now fix
r\in(0,1)
. If
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}_{*}||\leq r
then
r^{2}\geq||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}_{*}||^{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}(s_{i}-1)^{2}\geq(s_{i}-1)^{2},i\in\{1,\dots,d-2\}
. Therefore,
|s_{i}-1|\leq r\iff-r\leq s_{i}-1\leq r\iff 1-r\leq s_{i}\leq 1+r
. This implies

(1-r)^{d-2}\leq\prod\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}s_{i}\leq(1+r)^{d-2}.

Hence,

(1+r)^{-(d-2)}\leq(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}s_{i})^{-1}\leq(1-r)^{-(d-2)}\text{ i.e. }(1+r)^{-(d-2)}\leq s_{d-1}\leq(1-r)^{-(d-2)}.

We know for any vector
\boldsymbol{z}
,

\boldsymbol{z}^{T}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}+diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2}))\boldsymbol{z}\geq\boldsymbol{z}^{T}diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2})\boldsymbol{z}\geq(\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2})||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}.

Then,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))

\displaystyle=\min\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||=1}\boldsymbol{z}^{T}\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle\geq\min\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||=1}s_{d-1}(\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2})||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}

\displaystyle=s_{d-1}\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=s_{d-1}\min\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}s_{i}^{-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{d-1}}{(\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}s_{i})^{2}}.

On
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r
,
\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}s_{i}\leq 1+r
. Furthermore,
s_{d-1}\geq(1+r)^{-(d-2)}
. Then,

\lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))\geq\frac{(1+r)^{-(d-2)}}{(1+r)^{2}}=(1+r)^{-d+2}(1+r)^{-2}=(1+r)^{-d}.

Recall that for PSD matrices
A
and
B

\lambda_{\text{max}}(A+B)\leq\lambda_{\text{max}}(A)+\lambda_{\text{max}}(B)
. Then,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{max}}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\text{max}}(s_{d-1}[\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}+diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2})])

\displaystyle=s_{d-1}\lambda_{\text{max}}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T}+diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2}))

\displaystyle\leq s_{d-1}[\lambda_{\text{max}}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{w}^{T})+\lambda_{\text{max}}(diag(w_{1}^{2},\dots,w_{d-2}^{2}))]

\displaystyle=s_{d-1}[||\boldsymbol{w}||^{2}+\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2}].

Recall, on
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r,s_{i}\geq 1-r
. So
w_{i}=s_{i}^{-1}\leq(1-r)^{-1}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle||\boldsymbol{w}||^{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}w_{i}^{2}\leq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d-2}(\frac{1}{1-r})^{2}=\frac{d-2}{(1-r)^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(1-r)^{2}}.

Thus,

||\boldsymbol{w}||^{2}+\max\limits_{1\leq i\leq d-2}w_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{d-2}{(1-r)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(1-r)^{2}}=\frac{d-2+1}{(1-r)^{2}}=\frac{d-1}{(1-r)^{2}}.

Recall, on
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r
,
s_{d-1}\leq(1-r)^{-(d-2)}
. Thus,

\lambda_{\text{max}}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))\leq(1-r)^{-(d-2)}\frac{d-1}{(1-r)^{2}}=(d-1)(1-r)^{-d+2}(1-r)^{-2}=(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}.

Hence, on
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r
,

(1+r)^{-d}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))\leq\lambda_{\max}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}))\leq(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}.

Let
\Delta:=\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
and recall that
\boldsymbol{s}^{*}=\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}
. Let
g(t):=A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}),t\in[0,1]
. By Taylor’s integral remainder theorem,

\displaystyle g(1)

\displaystyle=g(0)+\frac{g^{\prime}(0)}{1!}(1-0)+\int\limits_{0}^{1}\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(t)}{1!}(1-t)^{1}dt

\displaystyle=g(0)+g^{\prime}(0)+\int\limits_{0}^{1}(1-t)g^{\prime\prime}(t)dt.

Note

\displaystyle\frac{dg}{dt}=\frac{d}{dt}{A}(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta})

\displaystyle=\nabla{A}(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta})^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}

and

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}g}{dt^{2}}=\frac{d}{dt}(\nabla{A}(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta})^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{T}\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta})\boldsymbol{\Delta}.

Thus,

\displaystyle A(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})+\nabla A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})^{T}\boldsymbol{\Delta}+\int\limits_{0}^{1}(1-t)\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{T}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}))\Delta dt

\displaystyle\text{ i.e }A(\boldsymbol{s})-A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{1}(1-t)\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{T}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}))\boldsymbol{\Delta}dt.

Then by the eigenvalue bounds,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{T}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}))\boldsymbol{\Delta}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\text{min}}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}))||\boldsymbol{\Delta}||^{2}\geq(1+r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{\Delta}||^{2}

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{T}(\nabla^{2}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+t\boldsymbol{\Delta}))\boldsymbol{\Delta}

\displaystyle\leq(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{\Delta}||^{2}.

Observe that
\int\limits_{0}^{1}(1-t)dt=0.5
. Hence,

\frac{1}{2}(1+r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{\Delta}||^{2}\leq A(\boldsymbol{s})-A(\boldsymbol{s}^{*})\leq\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{\Delta}||^{2}

if and only if

(d-1)+\frac{1}{2}(1+r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||^{2}\leq A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq(d-1)+\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||^{2},

on
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r.
That is, on
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||\leq r
,

(d-1)+C_{-}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||^{2}\leq A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq(d-1)+C_{+}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||^{2},

C_{-}:=\frac{1}{2}(1+r)^{-d}\text{ and }C_{+}:=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(1-r)^{-d}.

Now we need to bound
Q(\boldsymbol{s})
which will allow us to bound
H(x)
. Recall

\displaystyle Q(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{d-1}-1}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\gamma_{\ell}},\gamma_{\ell}:=\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{d-1}-1,\ell=1,\dots,d-2.

Just like for
A(\boldsymbol{s})
consider the ball
B(r):=\{\boldsymbol{s}\in(0,\infty)^{d-2}:||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{1}_{d-2}||\leq r\}
. Again, this implies
1-r\leq s_{\ell}\leq 1+r
. Fix some
r_{0}\in(0,1)
. Then on
B(r_{0}),s_{\ell}\in[a,b]\forall\ell
, where
a:=1-r_{0}>0
and
b:=1+r_{0}<\infty
. Now, fix an
\ell
. On the interval
[a,b]\subset(0,\infty)
,
t\rightarrow t^{\gamma_{\ell}}
is continuous and monotone. This means for
\gamma_{\ell}\geq 0,a^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq s_{\ell}^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq b^{\gamma_{\ell}}
and
\gamma_{\ell}\leq 0,b^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq s_{\ell}^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq a^{\gamma_{\ell}}
. That is,

\min\{a^{\gamma_{\ell}},b^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}\leq s_{\ell}^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq\max\{a^{\gamma_{\ell}},b^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}.

Then

\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\min\{a^{\gamma_{\ell}},b^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}\leq\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\gamma_{\ell}}\leq\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\max\{a^{\gamma_{\ell}},b^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}.

That is
q_{-}\leq Q(\boldsymbol{s})\leq q_{+},\forall s\in B(r_{0}),
where
q_{-}:=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\min\{(1-r_{0})^{\gamma_{\ell}},(1+r_{0})^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}
and
q_{+}:=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\max\{(1-r_{0})^{\gamma_{\ell}},(1+r_{0})^{\gamma_{\ell}}\}
. Now,

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle=\int e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\geq\int\limits_{B}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\geq\int\limits_{B}e^{-x[(d-1)+C_{+}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||^{2}]}q_{-}d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=q_{-}e^{-(d-1)x}\int\limits_{B}e^{-xC_{+}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||^{2}}d\boldsymbol{s}.

Let
\boldsymbol{z}=\sqrt{x}(\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*})
. Then
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{s}^{*}+x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\boldsymbol{z}
. Then
d\boldsymbol{s}=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}I_{d-2}d\boldsymbol{z}
. Therefore,

|\det(\frac{d\boldsymbol{s}}{d\boldsymbol{z}})|=|\det(x^{-\frac{1}{2}}I_{d-2})|=x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}\text{ i.e. }d\boldsymbol{s}=x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}d\boldsymbol{z}.

Furthermore if
\boldsymbol{s}\in B
, then
||\boldsymbol{z}||=\sqrt{x}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||\leq\sqrt{x}r_{0}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{B}e^{-xC_{+}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}_{*}||^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x}}e^{-C_{+}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}d\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x}}e^{-C_{+}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}d\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}I(x),I(x):=\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x}}e^{-C_{+}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}d\boldsymbol{z}.

Observe that
I(x)
is monotone increasing in
x
. If
x_{2}\geq x_{1}
then
r_{0}\sqrt{x_{2}}\geq r_{0}\sqrt{x_{1}}
so
||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x_{1}}\subseteq||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x_{2}}
. Since the integrand is strictly positive
I(x_{2})\geq I(x_{1})
. By the monotone convergence theorem

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{||\boldsymbol{z}||\leq r_{0}\sqrt{x}}e^{-C_{+}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}dz

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d-2}}e^{-C_{+}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}dz

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-C_{+}t^{2}}dt,\text{ by Tonelli's theorem}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{C_{+}}})

\displaystyle=(\frac{\pi}{C_{+}})^{\frac{d-2}{2}}.

Then,
\exists x_{1}:\forall x\geq x_{1},I(x)\geq b_{0}>0
for some number
b_{0}
. Thus,

\exists b_{1}>0,\exists x_{1}:\forall x\geq x_{1},H(x)\geq q_{-}e^{-(d-1)x}x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}b_{0}=b_{1}x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x},b_{1}:=q_{-}b_{0}.

Now we need to upper bound
H(x)
. To do this we will split the integral over
B
and
B^{c}
. So,

\displaystyle H_{B}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{B}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\leq q_{+}e^{-(d-1)x}\int\limits_{B}e^{-xC_{-}||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||^{2}}d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\leq q_{+}e^{-(d-1)x}x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{d-2}}e^{-C_{-}||\boldsymbol{z}||^{2}}d\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=b_{2}x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}.

Recall that by the AM-GM inequality
A(\boldsymbol{s})\geq d-1
with equality if
\boldsymbol{s}=(1,\dots,1)=\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
. We now aim to show that that
\exists\eta>0:\forall\boldsymbol{s}\in B^{c},A(\boldsymbol{s})\geq d-1+\eta
. Fix
M>0
and consider
S_{M}=\{\boldsymbol{s}\in(0,\infty)^{d-2}:A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq M\}
. As
A
is continuous,
S_{M}
is closed. Now we need boundedness away from
0
and
\infty
. If
A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq M
, for each
i
,

s_{i}\leq\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}\leq A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq M.

So
s_{i}\leq M
. Also,
(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}\leq A(\boldsymbol{s})\leq M
which implies
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}\geq\frac{1}{M}
. Therefore
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}=s_{i}\prod\limits_{\ell\neq i}s_{\ell}\leq s_{i}M^{d-3}.
So
M^{-1}\leq\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}\leq s_{i}M^{d-3}
which implies
s_{i}\geq M^{-(d-2)}
. Thus
\forall\boldsymbol{s}\in S_{M},M^{-(d-2)}\leq s_{i}\leq M,\forall i
. Therefore,
S_{M}\subseteq[M^{-(d-2)},M]^{d-2}
which is compact. As
S_{M}
is closed
S_{M}
is compact as a closed subset of a compact set is also compact. Note that
B^{c}:=\{||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||>r_{0}\}
. Let
F:=\{||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||\geq r_{0}\}
. Then
B^{c}\subseteq F
. Now we will show that
\exists\eta>0
such that
A(\boldsymbol{s})\geq(d-1)+\eta,\forall\boldsymbol{s}\in F
. Assume no such
\eta
exists. Then
\inf\limits_{\boldsymbol{s}\in F}A(\boldsymbol{s})=d-1
. Then we can pick
\boldsymbol{s}^{(n)}\in F
such that
A(\boldsymbol{s}^{(n)})\downarrow d-1
. In particular,
\forall
sufficiently large
n
we have
A(\boldsymbol{s}^{(n)})\leq d
. Now consider
K:=F\cap S_{d}
. We know
\boldsymbol{s}^{(n)}\in K
,
\forall
sufficiently large
n
. We know
F
and
S_{d}
are closed, therefore
K
is closed. Since
S_{d}
is bounded,
K
is also bounded. Therefore
K
is compact. Since
K
is compact
\{\boldsymbol{s}^{(n)}\}\subseteq K
has a convergent subsequence
\boldsymbol{s}^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}\in K
. By continuity of
A,A(\bar{\boldsymbol{s}})=\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}A(\boldsymbol{s}^{n_{k}})=d-1
. From the AM-GM inequality we know
A(\boldsymbol{s})=d-1
iff
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
. Therefore
\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
. But
\bar{\boldsymbol{s}}\in K\subset F
and every point in
F
satisfies
||\boldsymbol{s}-\boldsymbol{s}^{*}||\geq r_{0}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{s}\neq\boldsymbol{s}^{*}
is a contradiction. Therefore
\inf\limits_{\boldsymbol{s}\in F}A(\boldsymbol{s})>d-1
. Let
\eta:=\inf\limits_{\boldsymbol{s}\in F}A(\boldsymbol{s})-(d-1)>0
. Therefore,
A(\boldsymbol{s})\geq(d-1)+\eta
. Now we are in a position to upper bound
H(x)
on
B^{c}
. Note that

\displaystyle e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{x}{2}((d-1)+\eta)}e^{-x(A(\boldsymbol{s})-\frac{(d-1)+\eta}{2})}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{x}{2}((d-1)+\eta)}e^{-\frac{x}{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})}.

Then,

\displaystyle H_{B^{c}}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{B^{c}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})ds

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}\int\limits_{B^{c}}e^{-\frac{x}{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{d-2}}e^{-\frac{x}{2}A(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}H(\frac{x}{2}).

Then,

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle=H_{B}(x)+H_{B^{c}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}+e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}H(\frac{x}{2}),K_{B}=b_{2}.

Let
M(x):=x^{\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}H(x)
. This implies

M(x)=x^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}H(x)\leq K_{B}+x^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}H(\frac{x}{2}).

Observe that

H(\frac{x}{2})=(\frac{x}{2})^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-\frac{(d-1)x}{2}}M(\frac{x}{2}).

Hence,

\displaystyle M(x)

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}+x^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}e^{-\frac{x}{2}(d-1+\eta)}(\frac{x}{2})^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-\frac{(d-1)x}{2}}M(\frac{x}{2})

\displaystyle=K_{B}+2^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-\frac{\eta x}{2}}M(\frac{x}{2}).

That is

M(x)\leq K_{B}+q(x)M(\frac{x}{2}),q(x):=2^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-\frac{\eta x}{2}}.

Choose
x_{0}
such that
2^{\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-\frac{\eta x_{0}}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then,

M(x)\leq K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}M(\frac{x}{2})\forall x\geq x_{0}.

Since
A(\boldsymbol{s})\geq 0
,
x\rightarrow H(x)
is nonincreasing in
x
. So for any
x\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]
,

H(x)\leq H(x_{0})
. Therefore

M(x)=x^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}H(x)\leq(2x_{0})^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{2(d-1)x_{0}}H(x_{0}).

Let
M_{*}:=(2x_{0})^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{2(d-1)x_{0}}H(x_{0})
. Now we need to prove
M_{*}
is finite. Once this is done we can utilize a recursive application of the
M(x)\leq K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}M(\frac{x}{2})
inequality which will yield an upper bound on
H(x)
. Recall
H(x)=\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{d-2}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}
where

A(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}+(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}
and
Q(\boldsymbol{s})=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}},\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
. For each subset

J\subseteq\{1,\dots,d-2\}
let
R_{J}=\{\boldsymbol{s}:0<s_{i}<1,\text{ for }i\in J,s_{i}\geq 1\text{ for }i\in J^{c}\}
. Then the
2^{d-2}
sets are disjoint and their union is
(0,\infty)^{d-2}
. So
H(x)=\sum\limits_{J}\int\limits_{R_{J}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}
. On
R_{\phi}=[1,\infty)^{d-2},A(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}+(\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell})^{-1}\geq\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}
. Then
e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}\leq e^{-x\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}}
since
x>0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{R_{\phi}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{[1,\infty)^{d-2}}e^{-x\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-xs_{\ell}}s_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}ds_{\ell}.

Let
g(s)={s}^{-N}e^{\frac{x}{2}{s}}
. Then

\log(g({s}))=\frac{x}{2}{s}-N\log({s})\rightarrow\infty\text{ as }s\rightarrow\infty.

Therefore
\exists S
such that
\forall s\geq S,\log(g(s))\geq 0.
Then
g(s)\geq e^{0}=1,\forall s\geq S
. Then,
\exists S
such that
\forall s\geq S,s^{-N}e^{\frac{x}{2}s}\geq 1
. That is
s^{N}\leq e^{\frac{x}{2}s}
. If
s\geq 1
and
N>\beta
then
s^{N-\beta}\geq 1.
Then,
s^{\beta}s^{N-\beta}\geq s^{\beta}
. That is
s^{\beta}\leq s^{N}
. Then
e^{-xs}s^{\beta}\leq e^{-xs}s^{N}\leq e^{-xs}e^{\frac{x}{2}s}=e^{-\frac{x}{2}s}
. This implies

\displaystyle\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-xs}s^{\beta}ds

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{1}^{S}e^{-xs}s^{\beta}ds+\int\limits_{S}^{\infty}e^{-xs}s^{\beta}ds

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{1}^{S}e^{-xs}s^{\beta}ds+\int\limits_{S}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{x}{2}s}ds

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{1}^{S}e^{-xs}s^{\beta}ds+\frac{2}{x}e^{-\frac{x}{2}S}<\infty.

This implies
\int\limits_{R_{\phi}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}<\infty
. For a non-empty
J\subset\{1,\dots,d-2\}
with

|J|=k\geq 1
. Let
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{\ell})_{\ell\in J^{c}}\in[1,\infty)^{d-2-k}
and
\boldsymbol{u}=(u_{j})_{j\in J}\in(0,1)^{k}
. So on
R_{J},s=(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u})
and
\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{d-2}s_{\ell}=(\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell})(\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j})
On
R_{J}
, all
u_{j}>0
so
\sum\limits_{j\in J}u_{j}\geq 0
. Hence

\displaystyle A(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}+\sum\limits_{j\in J}u_{j}+(\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j})^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}+(\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j})^{-1}.

Since
x>0
,

\exp\{-xA(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{u})\}\leq\exp\{-x\sum\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\}\exp\{-x(\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j})^{-1}\}.

So

\int\limits_{R_{J}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}\leq\int\limits_{[1,\infty)^{d-2-k}}\exp\{-x\sum\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\}(\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}})I_{J}(c(\boldsymbol{v}))d\boldsymbol{v},

by Tonelli’s theorem, where

c_{v}:=x\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}^{-1}\in(0,x]\text{ and }I_{J}(c):=\int\limits_{(0,1)^{k}}\exp\{-c(\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j})^{-1}\}\prod\limits_{j\in J}u_{j}^{\beta_{j}}d\boldsymbol{u}.

Let
t_{j}=u_{j}^{-1}\in[1,\infty)
. Then
u_{j}=t_{j}^{-1},du_{j}=-t_{j}^{-2}dt_{j}
, and
u_{j}^{\beta_{j}}=t_{j}^{-\beta_{j}}
. Furthermore
(\prod u_{j})^{-1}=\prod t_{j}
. Then,

I_{J}(c)=\int\limits_{[1,\infty)^{k}}\exp\{-c\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{-(\beta_{j}+2)}d\boldsymbol{t}.

Let
M_{j}:=\max\{0,-(\beta_{j}+2)\}\geq 0
. Since
t_{j}\geq 1,t_{j}^{-(\beta_{j}+2)}\leq t_{j}^{M_{j}}.
Hence

I_{J}(c)\leq\int\limits_{[1,\infty)^{k}}\exp\{-c\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{M_{j}}d\boldsymbol{t}.

Let
S_{\ell}:=\{\boldsymbol{t}\in[1,\infty)^{k}:t_{\ell}=\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k}t_{j}\}
. On
S_{\ell}
,
\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\geq t_{\ell}
with
[1,\infty)^{k}=\bigcup\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}S_{\ell}
. So

\exp\{-c\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\}\leq\exp\{-ct_{\ell}\}\text{ and }\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{M_{j}}\leq t_{\ell}^{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}}.

Therefore,

\int\limits_{S_{\ell}}\exp\{-c\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{M_{j}}d\boldsymbol{t}\leq\int\limits_{S_{\ell}}e^{-ct_{\ell}}t_{\ell}^{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}}d\boldsymbol{t}.

For fixed
t_{\ell}=t
, the remaining
k-1
coordinates lie in
[1,t]^{k-1}
whose volume is

(t-1)^{k-1}\leq t^{k-1}
. So,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{S_{\ell}}\exp\{-c\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}^{M_{j}}d\boldsymbol{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-ct}t^{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}}t^{k-1}dt

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-ct}t^{P}dt,P:=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}+k-1\geq 0.

Then,

\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-ct}t^{P}dt\leq\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ct}t^{P}dt.

Let
u=ct
then
t=c^{-1}u
and
dt=c^{-1}du
. So

\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ct}t^{P}dt=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-u}(\frac{u}{c})^{P}\frac{du}{c}=c^{-(P+1)}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-u}u^{P+1-1}du=c^{-(P+1)}\Gamma(P+1).

Then

I_{J}(c)\leq\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{k}c^{-(P+1)}\Gamma(P+1)=k\Gamma(P+1)c^{-(P+1)}.

That is

I_{J}(c)\leq C_{J}c^{-q_{J}},q_{J}=P+1=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}M_{j}+k,C_{J}=k\Gamma(P+1).

Recall
c(\boldsymbol{v})=x\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}^{-1}
. This gives

I_{J}(c(\boldsymbol{v}))\leq C_{J}c(\boldsymbol{v})^{-q_{J}}=C_{J}x^{-q_{J}}\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}^{q_{J}}.

So,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{R_{J}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle\leq C_{J}x^{-q_{J}}\int\limits_{[1,\infty)^{d-2-k}}\exp\{-x\sum\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}\}\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}d\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=C_{J}x^{-q_{J}}\prod\limits_{\ell\in J^{c}}\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}.

Note

t^{N}\leq e^{\frac{x}{2}t},N>0\iff\log(t^{N})\leq\log(e^{\frac{x}{2}t})\iff N\log(t)\leq\frac{x}{2}t\iff 0\leq\frac{x}{2}t-N\log(t).

Let

h(t):=\frac{x}{2}t-N\log(t),t\geq 1\implies h^{\prime}(t)=\frac{x}{2}-\frac{N}{t}.

Let
T_{1}:=x^{-1}2N
. Then for any
t\geq T_{1}
,

h^{\prime}(t)=\frac{x}{2}-\frac{N}{t}\geq\frac{x}{2}-\frac{N}{T_{1}}=\frac{x}{2}-\frac{N}{\frac{2N}{x}}=\frac{x}{2}-\frac{x}{2}=0.

So for
t\geq T_{1},h(t)
is nondecreasing. Furthermore,

\frac{h(t)}{t}=\frac{x}{2}-N\frac{\log(t)}{t}\rightarrow\frac{x}{2}>0.

Since
h(t)\rightarrow\infty,\exists T_{2}
such that
h(T_{2})\geq 0
. Now set
T:=\max\{1,T_{1},T_{2}\}
. Since
T\geq T_{1},h
is nondecreasing on
[T,\infty)
. Since
T\geq T_{2},h(T)\geq 0
. Therefore,
\forall t\geq T,h(t)\geq h(T)\geq 0
. This means
\forall t\geq T,

\frac{x}{2}t-N\log(t)\geq 0\iff N\log(t)\leq\frac{x}{2}t\iff t^{N}\leq e^{\frac{x}{2}t}.

Since
t\geq 1
and for
N>\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}
we have
t^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}\leq t^{N}
. Hence,

e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}\leq e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{N}\leq e^{-xv_{\ell}}e^{(\frac{x}{2})v_{\ell}}=e^{-(\frac{x}{2})v_{\ell}}.

Then,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{1}^{\infty}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{1}^{T}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}+\int\limits_{T}^{\infty}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{1}^{T}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}+\int\limits_{T}^{\infty}e^{-(\frac{x}{2})v_{\ell}}dv_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{1}^{T}e^{-xv_{\ell}}v_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}+q_{J}}dv_{\ell}+\frac{2}{x}e^{-\frac{x}{2}T}

\displaystyle<\infty.

Thus,
\int\limits_{R_{J}}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}<\infty
for every nonempty
J
. Therefore each region is finite and there are only finitely many subsets of
J\subseteq\{1,\dots,d-2\}
. Therefore, summing over finitely regions we have finite integrals. Thus
H(x)<\infty
for
x>0
. Therefore

M_{*}=(2x_{0})^{\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{2(d-1)x_{0}}H(x_{0})<\infty.

Then
M(x)\leq M_{*}\forall x\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]
. Fix any
x\geq x_{0}
. Then choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x2^{-k}\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]
. Particularly, let
r:=x_{0}^{-1}x\geq 1
. Then

\frac{x}{2^{k}}\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]\iff 1\leq\frac{x}{2^{k}x_{0}}\leq 2\iff 2^{k}\leq r\leq 2^{k+1}.

Let

K:=\lfloor\log_{2}(\frac{x}{x_{0}})\rfloor.

Then,

K\leq\log_{2}(\frac{x}{x_{0}})<K+1.

So,

2^{K}\leq\frac{x}{x_{0}}\leq 2^{K+1}\iff 2^{K}x_{0}\leq x\leq 2^{K+1}x_{0}\iff x_{0}\leq\frac{x}{2^{K}}\leq 2x_{0}.

So we have
M(x)\leq K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}M(\frac{x}{2})
for
x\geq x_{0},M(x)\leq M_{*}\forall x\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]
, and

2^{-k}x\in[x_{0},2x_{0}]
. Therefore

\displaystyle M(x)

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}M(\frac{x}{2})

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}(K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}M(\frac{x}{4}))

\displaystyle=K_{B}+\frac{1}{2}K_{B}+\frac{1}{4}M(\frac{x}{4})

\displaystyle\leq K_{B}(1+\frac{1}{2}+\dots\frac{1}{2^{K-1}})+\frac{1}{2^{K}}M(\frac{x}{2^{K}}).

We know that
\sum\limits_{j=0}^{K-1}2^{-j}=2-2^{-K+1}\leq 2.
So,

M(x)\leq 2K_{B}+\frac{1}{2^{K}}M(\frac{x}{2^{K}})\leq 2K_{B}+\frac{1}{2^{K}}M_{*}.

Since
K\geq 0\iff-K\leq 0\implies 2^{-K}\leq 2^{0}=1
. Therefore,

M(x)\leq 2K_{B}+M_{*}\implies\sup\limits_{x\geq x_{0}}M(x)\leq 2K_{B}+M_{*}<\infty.

Recall
M(x)=x^{\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{(d-1)x}H(x)
. This implies

\displaystyle H(x)

\displaystyle=x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}M(x)

\displaystyle\leq(2K_{B}+M_{*})x^{-\frac{(d-2)}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}

\displaystyle=\tilde{b}_{2}x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x},\tilde{b}_{2}:=2K_{B}+M_{*}.

Therefore we have shown that

b_{1}x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}\leq H(x)\leq\tilde{b}_{2}x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}.

That is,

H(x)\asymp x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}.

Recall

\pi(x|u,\boldsymbol{k})\propto(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}\}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}H(x).

For sufficiently large
x,H(x)\asymp x^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}e^{-(d-1)x}
which implies

\displaystyle K_{1}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1-\frac{d-2}{2}}(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-f_{u}(x)\}

\displaystyle\leq\pi(x|u,\boldsymbol{k})

\displaystyle\leq K_{2}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1-\frac{d-2}{2}}(1+Cx^{d-1})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\exp\{-f_{u}(x)\}

where

f_{u}(x):=(d-1)x+\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{d-1})}.

Let
m:=d-1,x_{u}=(\frac{u}{2C})^{\frac{1}{m+1}}
and
A_{u}:=Cx_{u}^{m}
. For
x=\lambda x_{u},\lambda>0
,

\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{m})}=\frac{u}{2(1+C\lambda^{m}x_{u}^{m})}=\frac{u}{2(1+\lambda^{m}A_{u})}=\frac{u}{2A_{u}}\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}.

We know
u(2A_{u})^{-1}=u(2Cx_{u}^{m})^{-1}.
Note,

x_{u}=(\frac{u}{2C})^{\frac{1}{m+1}}\iff x_{u}^{m+1}=\frac{u}{2C}\iff 2Cx_{u}^{m+1}=u.

This gives

\frac{u}{2A_{u}}=\frac{2Cx_{u}^{m+1}}{2Cx_{u}^{m}}=x_{u}.

Therefore

f_{u}(\lambda x_{u})=m\lambda x_{u}+\frac{x_{u}}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}=x_{u}\phi_{u}(\lambda),\phi_{u}(\lambda):=m\lambda+\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}.

Also,

\displaystyle A_{u}=Cx_{u}^{m}

\displaystyle=C(\frac{u}{2C})^{\frac{m}{m+1}}

\displaystyle=Cu^{\frac{m}{m+1}}(2C)^{-\frac{m}{m+1}}

\displaystyle=Cu^{\frac{m}{m+1}}2^{-\frac{m}{m+1}}C^{-\frac{m}{m+1}}

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{m}{m+1}}C^{1-\frac{m}{m+1}}u^{\frac{m}{m+1}}

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{m}{m+1}}C^{\frac{1}{m+1}}u^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\rightarrow\infty\text{ as }u\rightarrow\infty.

Therefore
\phi_{u}(\lambda)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)=m\lambda+\lambda^{-m}
. Observe that for
\lambda>0
,
\lim\limits_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)=\infty
and
\lim\limits_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)=\infty
. Note that

\phi_{\infty}^{\prime}(\lambda)=m-m\lambda^{-m-1}.

Setting equal to zero gives

0=m-m\lambda^{-m-1}\iff m=m\lambda^{-m-1}\iff 1=\lambda^{-m-1}\iff\lambda=1.

Furthermore,

\phi^{\prime\prime}_{\infty}(\lambda)=-m(-m-1)\lambda^{-m-2}=m(m+1)\lambda^{-m-2}>0,\forall\lambda>0.

Therefore,

\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon):=\inf\limits_{|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon}(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))>0.

Now we will show that for fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1),\exists u_{0}
and
c(\epsilon)>0
such that
\forall u\geq u_{0},

\inf\limits_{|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon}(\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1))\geq c(\epsilon)>0.

Set
K:=\phi_{\infty}(1)+2\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)
. We know that
\lim\limits_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)=\infty
. For every number
K,\exists R>0
such that
\lambda\geq R\implies\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)\geq K
. We also know that
\lim\limits_{\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}}\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)=\infty
. Then,
\exists\delta>0
such that
0<\lambda\leq\delta\implies\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)\geq K
. Let
M_{0}:=\delta^{-1}
then
\lambda\leq M_{0}^{-1}\iff\lambda\leq\delta
. Thus, if
0<\lambda\leq M_{0}^{-1}
then
\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)\geq K
. Now let
M:=\max\{R,M_{0},1\}
. Then
M>1
. If
\lambda\geq M
then
\lambda\geq R
so
\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)\geq K
. If
0<\lambda<M^{-1}
then
\lambda\leq M^{-1}\leq M_{0}^{-1}
. Hence
\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)\geq K
. Therefore, when
\lambda\notin[M^{-1},M]
i.e.
\lambda\geq M
or
\lambda\leq M^{-1}
,

\phi_{\infty}\geq K=\phi_{\infty}(1)+2\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)
. This implies
\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1)\geq 2\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)
. On
[M^{-1},M],

\phi_{u}(\lambda)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)
uniformly because

\displaystyle|\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}-\lambda^{-m}|

\displaystyle=|\frac{\lambda^{m}}{\lambda^{m}(\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}})}-\frac{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}{\lambda^{m}(\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}})}|

\displaystyle=|\frac{-\frac{1}{A_{u}}}{\lambda^{m}(\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}})}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{A_{u}}}{\lambda^{m}(\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{A_{u}\lambda^{m}(\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}(A_{u}\lambda^{m}+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}}\cdot\frac{1}{A_{u}\lambda^{m}+1}.

If
\lambda\in[M^{-1},M]
then
\lambda\geq M^{-1}
. Therefore
\lambda^{m}\geq M^{-m}
which implies
\lambda^{-m}\leq M^{m}
. Since
A_{u}\lambda^{m}+1\geq A_{u}\lambda^{m},(A_{u}\lambda^{m}+1)^{-1}\leq(A_{u}\lambda^{m})^{-1}\leq A_{u}^{-1}M^{m}
. Therefore,

|\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}}|\leq(M^{m})(\frac{M^{m}}{A_{u}})=\frac{M^{2m}}{A_{u}}.

Thus,

\sup\limits_{\lambda\in[\frac{1}{M},M]}|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|\leq A_{u}^{-1}M^{2m}.

Then for sufficiently large
u,A_{u}
is large enough. So

\sup\limits_{\lambda\in[M^{-1},M]}|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|\leq\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{4}.

If
\lambda\in[M^{-1},M]
and
|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon
, then

\displaystyle\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)

\displaystyle=(\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda))+(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))+(\phi_{\infty}(1)-\phi_{u}(1))

\displaystyle=(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))+(\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda))-(\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1))

\displaystyle\geq(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))-|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|-|\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1)|

\displaystyle\geq\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)-|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|-|\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1)|,\text{ by definition of }\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)-\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{4}-\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{2}

\displaystyle>0.

That is
\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)>0
for
\lambda\in[M^{-1},M]
and
|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon.
Note that

\displaystyle\phi_{u}(1)

\displaystyle=m+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}

\displaystyle\leq m+1,\forall u,\text{ since }1+\frac{1}{A_{u}}\geq 1,\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}\leq 1.

Now, lower bound
\phi_{u}(\lambda)
when
\lambda\notin[M^{-1},M]
. For
\lambda\geq M,

\phi_{u}(\lambda)=m\lambda+\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}\geq m\lambda.

That is,
\phi_{u}(\lambda)\geq mM
if
\lambda\geq M
. Then,
\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq mM-(m+1)
. Then by choosing
M
large enough,
mM-(m+1)\geq c
for
c>0,\forall\lambda\geq M
. Now, consider
0<\lambda<M^{-1}
. If
0<\lambda<M^{-1}
, then
\lambda^{m}\leq M^{-m}
. Hence
\lambda^{m}+A_{u}^{-1}\leq M^{-m}+A_{u}^{-1}
. This implies

\frac{1}{\lambda^{m}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}\geq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{M^{m}}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}.

Now choose
u
large enough so
A_{u}\geq M^{m}
. Then
A_{u}^{-1}\leq M^{-m}
. So

\frac{1}{\frac{1}{M^{m}}+\frac{1}{A_{u}}}\geq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{M^{m}}+\frac{1}{M^{m}}}=\frac{M^{m}}{2}.

Therefore for large
u
such that
A_{u}\geq M^{m}
and
\lambda\leq M^{-1}
we have
\phi_{u}(\lambda)\geq 2^{-1}M^{m}
. This implies
\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq 2^{-1}M^{m}-(m+1)
. Then choosing M large enough

\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq C_{tail}:=\min\{mM-(m+1),2^{-1}M^{m}-(m+1)\}>0
. So we have that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\inf\limits_{|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon}(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))>0

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1)

\displaystyle\geq 2\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon),\text{ for }\lambda\notin[\frac{1}{M},M],

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\lambda\in[\frac{1}{M},M]}|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{4},\text{ which gives}

\displaystyle|\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{4},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)

\displaystyle\geq(\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1))-|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|-|\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1)|.

This gives

\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{2}>0.

Now choose
u_{0}
such that both large
u
inequalities hold. Consider
\lambda\in[M^{-1},M]
and
|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon
. By definition of
\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon),\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(1)\geq\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon).
Then

|\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{\infty}(\lambda)|\leq 4^{-1}\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)
and
|\phi_{u}(1)-\phi_{\infty}(1)|\leq 4^{-1}\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)
. Then
\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)>0
. For
\lambda\notin[M^{-1},M]
we know
\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq C_{tail}
. Therefore
\forall u\geq u_{0}
and
\forall\lambda
with
|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon,\phi_{u}(\lambda)-\phi_{u}(1)\geq C(\epsilon):=\min\{\frac{\Delta_{\infty}(\epsilon)}{2},C_{tail}\}>0
. Now take any
x>0
with
x\notin[(1-\epsilon)x_{u},(1+\epsilon)x_{u}].
Let
\lambda:=x_{u}^{-1}x>0
. Then
x=\lambda x_{u}
. This implies
x\notin[(1-\epsilon)x_{u},(1+\epsilon)x_{u}]\iff\lambda\notin[1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon]\iff|\lambda-1|\geq\epsilon.
Therefore

\displaystyle\phi_{u}(\lambda)\geq\phi_{u}(1)+c(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\iff x_{u}\phi_{u}(\lambda)\geq x_{u}\phi_{u}(1)+x_{u}c(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\iff f_{u}(x)=f_{u}(\lambda x_{u})\geq f_{u}(x_{u})+c(\epsilon)x_{u}.

That is
\forall\epsilon\in(0,1),\exists u_{0},c(\epsilon)>0:\forall u\geq u_{0},\forall x\notin[(1-\epsilon)x_{u},(1+\epsilon)x_{u}],

f_{u}(x)\geq f_{u}(x_{u})+c(\epsilon)x_{u}
. Fix
\epsilon\in(0,2^{-1})
. Recall

x_{u}=(\frac{u}{2C})^{\frac{1}{m+1}},A_{u}=Cx_{u}^{m}\text{ such that as }x_{u}\rightarrow\infty,A_{u}\rightarrow\infty.

Then
x_{u}A_{u}=Cx_{u}^{m+1}=2^{-1}u
. Let
\Pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(X\in\cdot|U=u,\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{k}).
We will prove that for large u

\Pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(|\frac{X}{x_{u}}-1|\geq\epsilon)\leq\exp\{-c(\epsilon)x_{u}\}.

Since
x_{0}
is fixed and
x_{u}\rightarrow\infty
, for all large
u
we know
(1-\epsilon)x_{u}\geq x_{0}
. Hence

\displaystyle\{|\frac{X}{x_{u}}-1|\geq\epsilon\}

\displaystyle=\{\{|\frac{X}{x_{u}}-1|\geq\epsilon\}\cap\{X\leq x_{0}\}\}\cup\{\{|\frac{X}{x_{u}}-1|\geq\epsilon\}\cap\{X\geq x_{0}\}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{X\leq x_{0}\}\cup\{\{|\frac{X}{x_{u}}-1|\geq\epsilon\}\cap\{X\geq x_{0}\}\}.

We will show that
\Pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}((0,x_{0}))
is negligible. Let

g(x):=\frac{1}{2(1+Cx^{m})}\text{ on }x\in(0,x_{0}),g(x)\geq g(x_{0})=:c_{0}>0.

Hence

\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{m})}\}=\exp\{-ug(x)\}\leq\exp\{-c_{0}u\}\text{ for }x\in(0,x_{0}).

Therefore up to a normalizing constant

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{x_{0}}\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{0}^{x_{0}}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}H(x)dx

\displaystyle=e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})[\int\limits_{0}^{x_{0}}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}dx]d\boldsymbol{s},\text{ by Tonelli's theorem,}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})[\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}(\frac{t}{A(\boldsymbol{s})})^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-t}\frac{dt}{A(\boldsymbol{s})}]d\boldsymbol{s},t=xA(\boldsymbol{s}),

\displaystyle=e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})[A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}t^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-t}dt]d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})[A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})]d\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})e^{-c_{0}u}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}d\boldsymbol{s}.

Recall

f_{T_{1},\dots,T_{m}|\boldsymbol{K}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m}|\boldsymbol{k})=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}t_{i}^{\alpha_{i}-1}e^{-t_{i}}.

Then under the reparameterization the joint density becomes

f_{X,\boldsymbol{S}|\boldsymbol{K}}(x,\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{k})=\frac{mx^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}.

Then the marginal density of
\boldsymbol{S}
is given by

\displaystyle f_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}x^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-xA(\boldsymbol{s})}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}(\frac{u}{A(\boldsymbol{s})})^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-u}\frac{du}{A(\boldsymbol{s})},u=xA(\boldsymbol{s}),

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}u^{\alpha_{\cdot}-1}e^{-u}du

\displaystyle=\frac{m\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}Q(\boldsymbol{s})A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}},\boldsymbol{s}\in(0,\infty)^{m-1}.

We know the density integrates to 1. So

1=\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}f_{\boldsymbol{S}}(\boldsymbol{s})d\boldsymbol{s}=\frac{m\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}d\boldsymbol{s}.

Therefore

\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{i})}{m\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})}=\int\limits_{(0,\infty)^{m-1}}Q(\boldsymbol{s})A(\boldsymbol{s})^{-\alpha_{\cdot}}d\boldsymbol{s}.

Hence

\displaystyle\int\limits_{0}^{x_{0}}\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{0}u}\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell})}{m\Gamma(\alpha_{\cdot})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell})}{m}e^{-c_{0}u}

\displaystyle=K_{0}e^{-c_{0}u},K_{0}:=\frac{\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{\ell})}{m}.

Note that

\displaystyle\gamma>-1

\displaystyle\iff\alpha_{\cdot}-1-\frac{m-1}{2}>-1\iff\alpha_{\cdot}-\frac{m-1}{2}>0

\displaystyle\iff\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}k_{\ell}-\frac{m-1}{2}>0\iff\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}k_{\ell}>\frac{m-1}{2}\iff\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}k_{\ell}>m-1.

Since each
k_{\ell}
is an integer
\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}k_{\ell}>m\implies\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{m}k_{\ell}>m-1.
Now fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and consider
I_{\delta}:=[x_{0}+1-\delta,x_{0}+1]\subset[x_{0},\infty).
On
I_{\delta}

(1+Cx^{m})^{-\frac{p}{2}}\geq(1+C(x_{0}+1)^{m})^{-\frac{p}{2}}=:c_{*},\exp\{-mx\}\geq\exp\{-m(x_{0}+1)\}.

Furthermore
H(x)\geq h_{*}=b_{1}\min\limits_{x\in I_{\delta}}(x^{-\frac{(m-1)}{2}}e^{-mx})
. Recall

\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x)\geq K_{1}x^{\gamma}(1+Cx^{m})^{-\frac{p}{2}}e^{-mx}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{m})}\}.

Let
A(x):=K_{1}x^{\gamma}(1+Cx^{m})^{-\frac{p}{2}}e^{-mx}
. Observe
A(x)
is a continuous, strictly positive function for
x>0
. Note that
I_{\delta}
is compact and bounded away from
0
. Then by the extreme value theorem
A_{\delta}:=\inf\limits_{x\in I_{\delta}}A(x)>0
. Then for every
x\in I_{\delta}
we have
K_{1}x^{\gamma}(1+Cx^{m})^{-\frac{p}{2}}e^{-mx}\geq A_{\delta}
. Therefore

\pi_{u,\boldsymbol{k}}(x)\geq A_{\delta}\exp\{-\frac{u}{2(1+Cx^{m})}\}.

On
I_{\delta},g(x)
is decreasing in
x
so for
x\in I_{\delta}
